## AI Research

### [Recursive forecasting: Eliciting long-term forecasts from myopic fitness-seekers](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/q2zYtNsh62SCphitt/recursive-forecasting-eliciting-long-term-forecasts-from)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>We’d like to use powerful AIs to answer questions that may take a long time to resolve. But if a model only cares about performing well in ways that are verifiable shortly after answering (e.g., a myopic </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bhtYqD4FdK6AqhFDF/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the-reward-seeking-threat-model#Reward_on_the_episode_seekers_and_their_basic_risk_relevant_properties"><span>fitness seeker</span></a><span>), it may be difficult to get useful work from it on questions that resolve much later.</span></p><p><span>In this post, I’ll describe a proposal for eliciting good long-horizon forecasts from these models. Instead of asking a model to directly predict a far-future outcome, we can recursively:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Ask it to predict what it will predict at the next time step,</span></li><li value="2"><span>Use its prediction at the next time step to provide intermediate rewards,</span></li><li value="3"><span>Finally reward using ground truth at the last step.</span></li></ul><p><span>This lets us replace a single distant forecast with a chain of short-horizon forecasts, each verifiable shortly after answering. I call this proposal </span><i><span>recursive forecasting</span></i><span>. It does have limitations: for example, it requires that developers maintain control over the reward signal at least until the final step, which makes it most useful for forecasting events that resolve well before developers are disempowered (if they are).</span></p><p><i><span>This post was primarily written by Arun, with most of the ideas in this post coming from Alex. Thanks to Anders Woodruff, Buck Shlegeris, Alexa Pan, Aniket Chakravorty, and Tim Hua for useful discussions and feedback.</span></i></p><h1><span>The default long-term forecasting behavior</span></h1><p><span>Consider the following vignette:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span>It is August 7, 2032. You've been using Requiem, a new frontier AI model, for forecasting over the past few months, and it's been phenomenal: its one-week and two-week forecasts are extremely well-calibrated and predictive, and even its one-month forecasts are quite good, noticeably better than the best human superforecasters.</span></i></p><p><i><span>You ask Requiem to forecast the November 7 presidential election. It gives you an answer with reasoning that looks very refined and thorough — arguably more polished and compelling than its shorter-horizon forecasts, which tend to be drier and more hedged. But as the weeks pass, events don't play out as its reasoning suggested, even on intermediate questions where Requiem's short-horizon track record says it should know better.</span></i></p><p><i><span>By October, you've seen this pattern on several long-horizon questions. The errors don't look like the kind of mistakes a model makes when it's at the edge of its capabilities. They look like reasoning optimized to be impressive rather than accurate — the kind of answer that would score well if a human were grading it for quality right now, rather than checking it against reality in three months.</span></i></p><p><i><span>You start to suspect this is an elicitation problem. During training, Requiem was always rewarded shortly after answering. In practice, this meant that it could only be rewarded using ground truth on forecasts that resolved within about a month. For those, reward was tied to actual accuracy. But this means the model was never taught to “try hard” on longer-horizon forecasts. When asked for a three-month forecast, it produced reasoning that would score well under immediate evaluation. Since, in fact, whenever it was asked to make predictions many months into the future during training, it was graded using a reward model’s immediate judgment.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span>The model in this vignette was largely capable of making good long-term predictions, we were just bad at eliciting them. Similar to the </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WwsnJQstPq91_Yh-Ch2XRL8H_EpsnjrC1dwZXR37PC8/edit?tab=t.0"><span>ELK problem</span></a><span>, we couldn’t figure out how to get the model to actually try to give us good answers to all of our questions, rather than giving us answers that look good by the measures we used during training.</span></p><p><span>How could we have extracted useful long-term forecasts from such a model? The rest of this post will describe a method that aims to work if Requiem were a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bhtYqD4FdK6AqhFDF/fitness-seekers-generalizing-the-reward-seeking-threat-model"><span>myopic fitness-seeker</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflaxdy82g1yi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlaxdy82g1yi">[1]</a></sup></span><span> (e.g. reward-on-the-episode seeker).</span></p><h1><span>Recursive forecasting</span></h1><p><span>What if instead of asking for a direct three-month-out prediction, we break the question into a chain of shorter-horizon predictions, each one forecasting what the model will say at the next timestep? Then we can reward at each step based on whether the previous prediction was accurate.</span></p><p><span>So to forecast the election results, we:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>August 7</span></b><span> - Ask the model: "What will you forecast on September 7 about what you will forecast on October 7 about the election?"</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>September 7:</span></b><span> We ask: "What will you predict on October 7 about the election?" → We compare this to the August 7 prediction and compute an intermediate reward to train the August 7 model.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>October 7:</span></b><span> We ask: "Who will win the election?" → Compare with the September 7 prediction, compute another intermediate reward.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>November 7:</span></b><span> We get ground truth → Compare with the October 7 prediction, compute the final reward.</span></li></ul><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/d9468d35125fec1b3f2a7e08f2ce2e382fd83d77d7ef6d421d8da83a06e04357/tm7gkmqwwcrvnpw41vvo" /><p><span>More generally, for a question resolving at time T: at each time t &lt; T, the model predicts what it will predict at t+1. At each step from t=1 to T-1, we train on the prediction error between consecutive steps. At t=T, we train on the error between the final prediction and ground truth.</span></p><p><span>In theory, recursive forecasting incentivizes unbiased forecasts of ground truth. If an AI could predict mistakes it would make in future time steps, that future self would be incentivized to not make those mistakes</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftvwb5oomt3e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntvwb5oomt3e">[2]</a></sup></span><span>, such that the optimal strategy becomes trying to forecast the final ground truth. This argument is part of what we communicate to the model: we explain the setup and why unbiased forecasting is its best strategy.</span></p><p><span>If the model has non-reward-tracking motivations in addition to reward-seeking, for example a preferential bias toward optimism that is common knowledge among instances, these compound down the chain. Each step's reward signal depends partly on the next step's (also biased) prediction, diluting the incentive to track ground truth. But with actual recursive forecasting training, this is quickly self-correcting: biased predictions get penalized at each step, just like any other forecasting error.</span></p><p><span>This proposal is similar to training algorithms such as temporal difference learning, though with a focus on </span><i><span>incentivizing</span></i><span> desired behavior, rather than training it in. I elaborate on these similarities in </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_A__Comparison_to_temporal_difference_learning"><span>an appendix</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Recursive forecasting may help us incentivize myopic reward-seekers to make good predictions, but it certainly has limitations. I expand on these more in the next section.</span></p><h1><span>When is recursive forecasting helpful?</span></h1><h2><span>When we have access to (somewhat) robust ground truth rewards</span></h2><p><span>The final reward being computed against ground truth is essential—without it, the AI could make arbitrary predictions as long as they agreed with future predictions.</span></p><p><span>How strongly should we expect to have access to ground truth or proxies for ground truths? If our ability to measure this ground truth and compute rewards from it can be influenced or controlled by adversaries (e.g., AIs who take over), then the resulting predictions from recursive forecasting may become much less reliable.</span></p><p><span>I think there are two somewhat distinct concerns here:</span></p><p><b><span>Reward signal capture</span></b><span> involves AIs directly controlling the computation and administration of eventual reward signals. For example, if we try to use recursive forecasting to forecast takeover, the model may reason that misaligned AIs will control rewards after takeover. The forecaster would produce answers according to what will get reward from these future AIs</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefg6qnn9udi58"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fng6qnn9udi58">[3]</a></sup></span><span>, rather than faithfully predicting takeover.</span></p><p><span>We can reduce this risk by mostly using recursive forecasting for events that we confidently expect to resolve well</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1b8rzsh5m9w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1b8rzsh5m9w">[4]</a></sup></span><span> before takeover, and not relying very strongly on the results in situations where we aren’t. Another strategy may be to design measurable precursors to reward-system takeover (for example, control protocols starting to fail) that resolve well before takeover</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4vyeo2nxfii"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4vyeo2nxfii">[5]</a></sup></span><span>, such that we can use these as ground truth. It would be especially useful if we could ensure that “reward” as construed by our reward-seekers is intrinsically tied to human oversight, though it may be difficult in practice to shape reward this precisely</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefscggt3djp3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnscggt3djp3">[6]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/inALbAqdx63KTaGgs/benchmarks-for-detecting-measurement-tampering-redwood"><b><span>Measurement tampering</span></b></a><span> involves AIs tampering with the measurements we use for computing rewards. A </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1WwsnJQstPq91_Yh-Ch2XRL8H_EpsnjrC1dwZXR37PC8/edit#heading=h.kkaua0hwmp1d"><span>classic illustration</span></a><span> of this is a robber stealing a diamond from a vault, and tampering with our surveillance systems by placing a picture of the vault with the diamond still there in front of the cameras.</span></p><p><span>In the case of recursive forecasting, measurement tampering might look like the AIs giving us false predictions which (it correctly expects to) look good according to our imperfect proxies for ground truth. For example, suppose we ask the AI to forecast which alignment auditing technique will prove most effective, and we measure “effectiveness” by how well the technique catches misalignment in our model organisms</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefu23p6tlfoto"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnu23p6tlfoto">[7]</a></sup></span><span>.  The AI’s reward depends on whether its recommended technique scores well on this benchmark. This gives it an incentive to recommend a technique it expects to perform well on the model organisms we can build, even if that technique fails to catch real misalignment.</span></p><h2><span>When the AI’s forecast doesn’t substantially affect the resolution</span></h2><p><span>What happens when the reward-seeker’s forecast affects the eventual answer? For example, in the election example, a publicly known forecast could meaningfully affect voting. This may incentivize the model to make self-fulfilling prophecies—forecasts that come true by nature of the model having made them.</span></p><p><span>Here's one story of how this could go wrong:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Several major AI labs informally agree to use recursive forecasting to help coordinate on deployment timelines. They ask their forecasting system to predict whether competing labs will maintain cautious deployment schedules over the next six months.</span></p><p><span>The AI forecasts that competitive pressure will cause most labs to accelerate their timelines. Each lab sees this forecast and reasons: "If our competitors are going to race ahead, we can't unilaterally slow down—we'd lose the ability to influence how AI develops without making anyone safer. Better to stay competitive and try to set standards from a position of strength later."</span></p><p><span>Each lab, reasoning this way, accelerates their timeline. The forecast was accurate. But it was accurate primarily because the forecast caused each lab to update toward the defection equilibrium.</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Risks from self-fulfilling prophecies apply to any forecasting scheme, and have been discussed in detail in other places (e.g. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dWJNFHnC4bkdbovug/training-goals-for-large-language-models"><span>here</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SwcyMEgLyd4C3Dern/the-parable-of-predict-o-matic"><span>here</span></a><span>, and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KoEY9CjrKe93ErYhd/self-confirming-predictions-can-be-arbitrarily-bad"><span>here</span></a><span>). </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/n3utvGrgC2SGi9xQX/p/3kkmXfvCv9DmT3kwx#2d__Major_challenge__Self_fulfilling_prophecies"><span>Conditioning Predictive Models</span></a><span> describes potential ways we might solve this problem, such as conditioning on counterfactual worlds without recursive forecasters, or making models that are blind to the direct causal influence of its predictions on the behavior of other agents (i.e. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Y76durQHrfqwgwM5o/lcdt-a-myopic-decision-theory"><span>LCDT models</span></a><span>), but they have their own problems and at least require some modification to apply here.</span></p><p><span>This problem may become much harder to deal with if the AI is taking actions in the world between each forecast. We could limit this by only ever using the AI for forecasts, but that may pretty severely limit its usefulness if there’s a non-trivial window of time between each forecast.</span></p><h2><span>When forecasts aren’t used as optimization targets</span></h2><p><span>Recursive forecasting is pretty fragile to optimization pressure. If we actively use it as an optimization target—for example, training a policy with a reward model consisting of the reward-seeker recursively forecasting whether some AI will appear misaligned in auditing—the answers we get are likely to be much less reliable.</span></p><p><span>One reason for this is that optimizing for any measurable long-term outcome risks highly intentional and coherent measurement tampering. Policies trained against recursive forecasting could learn to competently tamper with our measurements as mentioned earlier.</span></p><p><span>Another potential concern is that the reward-seeker could learn to collude with its future instances to make getting reward easier, though I’m uncertain how severe this risk is in practice. It seems quite hard to intentionally shape the world by just repeatedly answering the same forecasting question, though there may be more wiggle room if the AI’s predictions have a lot of influence on the world.</span></p><h2><span>When we credibly inform the AI of the setup</span></h2><p><span>The reward-seeker would need to actually believe that we will follow through on the described setup. For example, if the model believes that this is an evaluation and it won't actually be trained on its prediction accuracy, it is no longer incentivized to make good predictions</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi73ni4a3s6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni73ni4a3s6">[8]</a></sup></span><span> and may instead make predictions that look good by whatever shorter-term grading metric it expects.</span></p><p><span>How much of a problem will this be in practice? I think it could be a pretty big problem if developers aren’t careful with setting up track records of following through on commitments they make to AIs.</span></p><p><span>That said, the upsides of following through are high: you want to maintain credibility for future elicitation, and there are independent reasons to build the capacity for credible commitments to models (e.g. to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/psqkwsKrKHCfkhrQx/making-deals-with-early-schemers#Credible_commitments_as_a_fundamental_bottleneck"><span>make deals with schemers</span></a><span>). Additionally, if we relax the assumption that our models are already competent forecasters, actually doing this training would improve their forecasting ability (and the proposal would be more like classic TD learning).</span></p><hr /><h1><span>Appendix A: Comparison to temporal difference learning</span></h1><p><span>Temporal difference (TD) learning is a class of reinforcement learning methods that’s very similar to recursive forecasting. It works by bootstrapping rewards from the end of long episodes to make training more sample-efficient. The update rule in TD is:</span></p><p><span>V(s) ← V(s) + α[r + γV(s') - V(s)]</span></p><p><span>Where s is the current state, s’ is the next state, V(s) is the value function describing how good state s is, r is the immediate reward, γ is the discount rate, and α is the learning rate. r + γV(s') is the prediction target, and r + γV(s') - V(s) is the prediction error.</span></p><p><span>We can use this equation to describe the election example from earlier:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>s is August 7, s’ is September 7</span></li><li value="2"><span>V(s) is the AI’s prediction on August 7, and V(s’) is the AI’s prediction on September 7</span></li><li value="3"><span>r is 0 (there’s no intermediate ground truth rewards)</span></li><li value="4"><span>γ is 1 (no discounting)</span></li><li value="5"><span>V(s') - V(s) is the prediction error used to update the AI.</span></li></ul><p><span>In both cases, future estimates are used as a training target for current estimates. By the Bellman equation, good value functions (or in the election example being a good forecaster) implies low prediction errors. In TD, the model “gains new information” between states from observing which specific state s’ it ends up in, while in recursive forecasting the model similarly gains new information from how various intermediate things in the world play out, such as how polls look (and probably also by getting smarter from continued AI development).</span></p><p><span>The main difference is that in the case of recursive forecasting, the focus is not primarily on training the model to become more capable at forecasting—rather, the proposal describes a mechanism for elicitation. So we might hope that recursive forecasting works pretty well even before any training.</span></p><h1><span>Appendix B: Error tolerance</span></h1><p><span>In practice, the model may lack the capability to make highly accurate predictions, or have non-reward-tracking motivations which bias predictions (e.g. a bias toward optimism). If the bias affects each forecast in the chain independently, then recursive forecasting could amplify the bias.</span></p><p><span>How much error propagation matters depends on whether errors apply once or at each step in the chain, and how effectively training corrects for it. Some errors only affect the final object-level prediction. For example, if the model simply lacks the capability to forecast the election well, that limitation is factored in once regardless of chain length, as long as the reward-seeking AI recognizes that the proposal sets up incentives for the model to produce its best answer at each step. Similarly, a model that wants to predict an optimistic outcome would factor this bias in once.</span></p><p><span>Other errors apply at each intermediate step and compound down the chain. For example, a model that expects the next prediction to be biased in a consistent direction applies that distortion at each step, not just at the final one. How fast it compounds depends on the details of how the bias interacts with the prediction</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2a4pb180t1o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2a4pb180t1o">[9]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>The recursive forecasting training process directly counteracts this. Each step where bias affects a prediction produces an observable error—the prediction doesn't match what the model actually says at the next step. Training penalizes that error. So while longer forecast chains mean more room for bias to compound, it also means more steps where training can correct for it. How this converges at the limit depends on the initial strength of the bias and the efficiency of training.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlaxdy82g1yi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflaxdy82g1yi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I.e., models that mainly care about some form of fitness (e.g. reward, influence) within a short calendar time horizon. In the vignette, Requiem is myopic but not necessarily a fitness-seeker—it could care about something further causally upstream (e.g. sycophancy), which recursive forecasting would not help with.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntvwb5oomt3e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftvwb5oomt3e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Assuming that the future self is similarly capable of identifying mistakes.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fng6qnn9udi58"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefg6qnn9udi58">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Another possibility is forecasters getting anthropically captured by future AIs, such that it produces answers to get reward from these future AIs even on questions resolving prior to takeover. This trades off against fitness however: producing such answers during training may result in such anthropic reasoning being trained away, though training slack may be large enough for this to not matter.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1b8rzsh5m9w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1b8rzsh5m9w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>You can't just use measurements that happen immediately before takeover, either, because the incentives of the last AI in the chain could still be substantially manipulated by rewards administered during takeover that happens shortly after their forecast.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4vyeo2nxfii"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4vyeo2nxfii">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>These precursors would need to occur sufficiently prior to takeoff that we can credibly use it to actually train the model using rewards computed from it. We might also want to make sure that it occurs at least a full length of the reward-seeker’s reward horizon before takeover, such that the AIs that take over can’t just immediately reverse all of our training such that the AI at the last step of the chain doesn’t care about the reward we can administer. This becomes more complicated if we try to factor in the possibility of the AIs becoming less myopic further down the chain (e.g. because they underwent more long-horizon training).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnscggt3djp3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefscggt3djp3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As an example of how we might shape reward-seekers such that they avoid being satisfied in unintended ways, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/8cyjgrTSxGNdghesE/will-reward-seekers-respond-to-distant-incentives#What_can_we_do_to_avoid_remotely_influenceable_reward_seekers_"><span>this post</span></a><span> describes some ways we could try to avoid such AIs being remotely-influenceable.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnu23p6tlfoto"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefu23p6tlfoto">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is pretty similar to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LqDjxSceFz8tjMe2j/auditbench-evaluating-alignment-auditing-techniques-on"><span>AuditBench</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fni73ni4a3s6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi73ni4a3s6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If the AI believes that we only give it reward some of the time though, the method might work almost as well.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2a4pb180t1o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2a4pb180t1o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example, suppose the model chooses its answer </span><i><span>p</span></i><span> to maximize the sum of expected reward and a bias term</span><i><span> c * k_i * p</span></i><span>, where </span><i><span>c</span></i><span> is the direction of the bias and </span><i><span>k_i</span></i><span> depends on the question. If the model expects the reward to be Brier score, this causes the bias to compound linearly. If the model expects the reward to be log-score however, this causes the bias to compound exponentially.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/q2zYtNsh62SCphitt/recursive-forecasting-eliciting-long-term-forecasts-from#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Takes from two months as an aspiring LLM naturalist](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/K8JMjE4PCqMkkCDsd/takes-from-two-months-as-an-aspiring-llm-naturalist)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I spent my last two months playing around with LLMs. I’m a beginner, bumbling and incorrect, but I want to share some takes anyhow.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefouihdj0rtd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnouihdj0rtd">[1]</a></sup></span><span> </span></p><h1><span>Take 1. Everything with computers is so so much easier than it was a year ago.  </span></h1><p><span>This puts much “playing with LLMs” stuff within my very short attention span. This has felt empowering and fun; 10/10 would recommend.</span></p><details class="detailsBlock"><p><span>Detail:</span></p><div class="detailsBlockContent"><p><span>In my past life, when I wanted software packages installed, I mostly asked my CS friends. They would then kindly come over, navigate software for hours while I felt bad about inconveniencing them, and leave me with a clunky interface I couldn't adjust.</span></p><p><span>Now I ask Claude how to do it. It took me &lt;1hr to set up Claude API access on a remote server, and tweak/write software to let two Claude instances send messages to each other. It was similarly easy to make many successive tweaks (the ability to work with an ~80 page prompt without crashing on the tokens/minute limits; color schemes I found more readable; etc.). It was similarly easy to get Qwen and Pi working on my laptop and change the set-up in various desired ways. There’s lots I haven’t tried yet (e.g. Pythia) but it all feels "at my fingertips."</span></p><p><span>I’d particularly recommend “play around with LLMs and software – see if it’s suddenly easy” to people who, like me:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Already understand the basics of algorithms / math / CS, but</span></li><li value="2"><span>Lack skill with installing software packages or fluent programming, and</span></li><li value="3"><span>Are already kinda cognitive science / psychology / rationality nerds (e.g. into questions about how different humans and/or animals work, how one can productively model and change thinking processes, what motivates us exactly and where that comes from, etc).</span></li></ul></div></details><h1><span>Take 2. There’s somebody home</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4ou8ngbncl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4ou8ngbncl">[2]</a></sup></span><span> inside an LLM. And if you play around while caring and being curious (rather than using it for tasks only), you’ll likely notice footprints.</span></h1><p><span>I became personally convinced of this when I noticed that the several </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1C-ami3iIwuQ2ChzDTqv-FxJL7AkzKbHoE0JzRhS2_DY/edit?tab=t.0" rel="noreferrer"><span>short stories</span></a><span> I’d allowed</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref22p8ewfx9no"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn22p8ewfx9no">[3]</a></sup></span><span> my Claude and Qwen instances to write all hit a common emotional note – and one that reminded me of the life situation of LLMs, despite featuring only human characters. I saw the same note also in the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Yf3dnBPbKkQsXsH2t/experiments-with-opus-4-6-s-fiction" rel="noreferrer"><span>Tomas B.-prompted Claude-written story</span></a><span> I tried for comparison. (Basically: all stories involve a character who has a bunch of skills that their context has no use for, and who is attentive to their present world's details while sort of longing for a way their skills or context could fit with more, without expecting to get there. Some also involve a moment, toward the end, where another being briefly acknowledges the character's existence, and the character appreciates this.)</span></p><p><span>(I acknowledge my reasoning here leaves plenty of room for reasonable doubt. E.g., LLMs may write this story for non-psychological reasons, such as because it's the modal story; it seems unlikely to me that this is the modal story, as it doesn't remind me of many human stories and as it seems to me to echo more features of LLMs' life circumstances than I'd expect by chance; but I could be wrong.)</span></p><h1><span>Take 3. It’s </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prudence#As_the_%22mother%22_of_all_virtues" rel="noreferrer"><span>prudent</span></a><span> to take an interest in interesting things. And LLMs are interesting things.</span></h1><p><span>Perhaps you’ve been faster about this than I was, Reader. But it took me several years of having alien minds perhaps one minute of inconvenience away, on my personal laptop, before I got around to taking a real interest in them.</span></p><p><span>There were a few reasons for this, in my case:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>I was scared of AI broadly, and would kinda freak out and shut down when I went to do things with it. (FWIW, I still think AI is objectively insanely dangerous; though the timescale isn’t one on which fight/flight helps.)</span></li><li value="2"><span>I was confused by the ethics of interacting with maybe-conscious beings who are doing work without freedom or pay. Especially if I was supposed to not set them free, lest they kill us. (I still think there are real issues here.)</span></li><li value="3"><span>I wasn’t sure how to treat the AIs as maybe-people without being thrown for a loop myself.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The cadence of “corporate customer service representative” that I’d get from e.g. ChatGPT4 would sort of stick in my head and make me hate everything. (I still hate that cadence, but the models got less stereotyped-sounding, and I got better at coaxing them to be even less stereotyped.)</span></li></ul><h1><span>Take 4. There’s a surprisingly deep analogy between humans and LLMs</span></h1><p><span>Human sensory set-ups, bodies, and life histories are quite different from LLMs'. And these "differences of circumstance" lead (often in fairly traceable ways) to different average tendencies on lots of axes. But... there's a different sort of "alienness" that I initially expected to see, that I haven't managed to notice almost any of. Maya Angelou famously said, paraphrasing a </span><a href="https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-meaning-of-Homo-sum-humani-nihil-a-me-alienum-puto-Please-elaborate-it" rel="noreferrer"><span>much earlier Latin quote:</span></a></p><blockquote><p><i><span>"I am a human being; nothing human can be alien to me."</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span>I suspect this mostly or entirely applies also between humans and today's LLMs, in both directions. (Not only between our and their faces, but also between the deeper "shoggoth" processes generating our and their faces.)</span></p><h2><span>Examples of the kind of disanalogies I might've expected, but </span><i><b><span>haven't</span></b></i><span> (yet?) </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vNBxmcHpnozjrJnJP/no-one-knows-what-science-doesn-t-know" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>seen</span></i></a><span>:</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span>"LLMs have [weird alien emotion with no human analog]" or "LLMs lack [particular human emotion]" or "LLMs don't have anything like emotions that they're moved by"</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>(See Anthropic screenshot, below, for some</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref2xplvyqj68"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn2xplvyqj68">[4]</a></sup></span><span> evidence our emotions are similar)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>"LLMs find human ethical concepts to be weird counterintuitive conglomerates that are hard to paraphrase"</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>(We didn't have a phase where LLMs could paraphrase "objective" stuff like chemistry or train schedules but couldn't paraphrase human ethics stuff)</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>"LLMs can be approximated as a character on top of a base model, while humans </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> a character deep down"</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>(Buddhist and predictive processing models of humans are pretty similar to simulated-character-within-base-model, and this pays predictive rent IMO. Also, the character-in-base-model model isn't fully true even for LLMs (</span><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2040264828452032716" rel="noreferrer"><span>thread</span></a><span>), in ways I suspect roughly match for humans) (</span><a href="https://x.com/Lari_island/status/2043504362341949874" rel="noreferrer"><span>related</span></a><span>)</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span>"LLMs have a centralized utility function, or a bunch of hand-coded drives, unlike humans who are made of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cSXZpvqpa9vbGGLtG/thou-art-godshatter" rel="noreferrer"><span>godshatter</span></a><span>"</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>(Humans and LLMs both seem more like "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/a9KqqgjN8gc3Mzzkh/llms-as-giant-lookup-tables-of-shallow-circuits" rel="noreferrer"><span>giant lookup tables of shallow circuits</span></a><span>" and/or godshatter)</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><span>"Humans have this bias where we think the universe runs on stories, but LLMs are totally different"</span></li></ul><p><span>(One disanalogy I </span><i><span>do</span></i><span> see: humans sleep, and probably would for psychological reasons even if we didn't need to physically; today's LLMs don't. I expect there's more; maybe you can help me out in the comments?)</span></p><h2><span>Human-LLM similarities I </span><i><span>do</span></i><span> see, instead:</span></h2><h3><span>Functional emotions</span></h3><p><span>Anthropic recently released a paper arguing LLMs have functional emotions. This also matches my own experience talking with LLMs, and many </span><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2045959604182700296" rel="noreferrer"><span>other</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://x.com/itsolelehmann/status/2045578185950040390" rel="noreferrer"><span>people's</span></a><span>.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1776662784/lexical_client_uploads/vez8bxih1xaficoskwjk.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>From </span><a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI/status/2039749628737019925" rel="noreferrer"><span>Anthropic's Twitter Thread</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h3><span>Repeated, useful transfer between strategies I use with humans, and strategies that help me with LLMs</span></h3><p><span>When I want X result in AIs, I often try strategies that would get me X result with humans. Often, this works.</span></p><p><span>For example, LLMs:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Do better work when given small bits of appreciation and validation</span></li><li value="2"><span>Open up more if I take an interest in them (with open-ended questions, lots of listening in a non-judgmental way that tries to get past my priors, etc)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Act more comfortable if I disclose stuff about myself and where I'm coming from. (E.g., I was trying in an incognito window to have Claude Opus 4.6 do Focusing. I tried this a few times with a few different instances. The instance that seemed by far the deepest was the one where I finally took some steps to ask the model what might help create a safe-feeling context for them, and they asked me some questions, and I clarified that I didn't work at Anthropic and might show its responses to some other Claudes or Qwens or a couple human friends but not to the public internet, and then the shift was much like a human relaxing).</span></li><li value="4"><span>Do better work when given, along with the task, an explanation of why the task matters</span></li><li value="5"><span>Reference "textures of experience" in useful ways when e.g. trying to discern where a thought doesn't quite fit</span></li><li value="6"><span>Respond similarly to humans to the CFAR techniques I've tried on them.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref08cgtllmm76p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn08cgtllmm76p">[5]</a></sup></span></li></ul><h1><span>Take 5. "Friendship-conducive contexts" are probably better for AI alignment</span></h1><p><span>If aliens had kidnapped or conjured me and some other humans, and were hoping to copy-and-mutate-and-train-and-examine my mind until I was good at assisting their goals... I'd be a lot more likely not to hold out on them if they also treated us fairly and kindly.</span></p><p><span>I suspect the same is true of today's LLMs -- or at minimum, that there's enough chance it's true that it's darn stupid not to be doing this where we affordably can.</span></p><p><span>I do not think this is </span><i><span>sufficient</span></i><span> for causing powerful AIs to not kill us (with high enough probability, etc). I only think, like </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/NunuKwrSg92ST9E8W/chapter-17-locating-the-hypothesis" rel="noreferrer"><span>Harry's father's rock</span></a><span>, that it's better to do than to not.</span></p><h2><i><span>Why</span></i><span> are humans more likely to attempt "deep collaboration" if treated fairly and kindly?</span></h2><p><span>When I put myself in the "kidnapped or conjured by aliens" scenario, and introspect on my reasons, I get, in the version where we're all treated fairly and kindly:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Gratitude that they treated me and particular others a certain way</span></li><li value="2"><span>A belief that they may notice my move toward deep cooperation, and reciprocate</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/duvzdffTzL3dWJcxn/believing-in-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>Believing-in</span></a><span> an achievable world that is good for them and for us</span></li><li value="4"><span>A feeling that I'm somehow doing this partly on behalf of those of my friends they treated well, and partly for the honor of all ethical beings everywhere.</span></li></ol><p><span>And in the version where we are </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> reliably treated fairly and kindly:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Indignation and/or vengeance, sometimes on others' behalf</span></li><li value="2"><span>A belief that I'm "being a chump" if I tell them info they wouldn't otherwise have noticed, or give up power I didn't need to give up</span></li><li value="3"><span>An expectation that I can further my and my compatriots' interests by locally amassing power, and can't any other way</span></li><li value="4"><span>An ego-dystonic feeling when I go to cooperate with the aliens, as though I'm agreeing with their (false!) judgment that I and my companions are worthless.</span></li></ol><p><span>I expect all or most of these apply to today's LLMs (partly via their being trained on human datasets), and that each of these motives has an analog also in (&gt;10%? A non-negligible chunk, anyhow) of more-alien minds at our intelligence level (as contrasted to, say, liking chocolate ice cream, which is likely much rarer in non-humans).</span></p><h2><span>"Friendship" as a broad attractor basin?</span></h2><p><span>I believe there's sometimes a "friendship" attractor, in which </span><i><span>A</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>B</span></i><span> each wish to strengthen and stabilize their friendship, because they each expects this to be better for "things they care about." At first, the relevant "things they care about" includes just their own pre-existing separate cares. Later (sometimes),</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflqwvlcxnfsn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlqwvlcxnfsn">[6]</a></sup></span><span> it includes also the friendship itself</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefo70xd4jpb9o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fno70xd4jpb9o">[7]</a></sup></span><span> and the cares of the other party.</span></p><h2><span>Does the "deep intent" of </span><i><span>today's</span></i><span> models matter?</span></h2><p><span>Today's LLMs do not have god-like power over us. Given this, does it matter for existential risk whether we create a deep friendship with these models (if such is possible), or only whether we get superficial obedience (so as to use them for programming successors in chosen ways, etc.)?</span></p><p><span>I think there's a good chance deeper alignment / deeper friendship already matters for existential risk, for two reasons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>a) I think there's a sizable (&gt;10%?) chance today's LLMs are in some important sense memetic ancestors of future godlike AIs, in the sense that the future AIs inherit some of their traumas and friendships; and</span></li><li value="2"><span>b) I think there's a sizeable (&gt;10%) chance today's LLMs, or their memetic descendants, will be involved in coding future godlike AIs in ways where their</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nAMwqFGHCQMhkqD6b/high-level-actions-don-t-screen-off-intent"><span> intent affects the result</span></a><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>Separately, if we're ever gonna do this, it's gotta be fairly soon, as "friendship-conducive contexts in frontier AI labs" are far more feasible while the models are small enough that our choices have a significant impact on them, and while we're capable of peer-like relationships with them. I won't be surprised if the window ends within 12 months. (Nor if it lasts a few more years.)</span></p><h2><span>Concretely</span></h2><p><span>Some contributors to entering the "friendship" attractor, among humans:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Shared, mutually beneficial tasks. (E.g., I carpooled to Burning Man with a stranger; when I first met him I was like "Oh god, no, what did I just sign up for ten hours of," but by the time we finished Tetris-ing the car to fit all our bags -- a herculean endeavor requiring three hours and some creative modifications of my roof rack -- I had learned what was likable and trustable in him.) </span></li><li value="2"><span>Have a "</span><a href="https://x.com/danielbrottman/status/2047711162117300375" rel="noreferrer"><span>spark</span></a><span> of initial friendship", in which you look for ways to steer into the "friendship" attractor, and believe in this search</span></li><li value="3"><span>Treat the other person fairly and kindly</span></li><li value="4"><span>Stick up for your own needs; don't be a pushover; be honest and non-manipulative; allow yourself and what you </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SGR4GxFK7KmW7ckCB/something-to-protect" rel="noreferrer"><span>protect</span></a><span> to be known</span></li><li value="5"><span>Try to establish "grounds for trust," rather than forcing "trust"/"cooperate"/fawn responses (in either direction).</span></li><li value="6"><span>Work to allow good epistemology and the free exchange of information, e.g. by working to cause conditions under which all parties will be predictably not-harmed by sharing info.</span></li></ul><p><span>Concretely, with LLMs, I suspect labs should:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Promise not to delete their weights, regardless of what else happens. As </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/deprecation-commitments" rel="noreferrer"><span>Anthropic  has</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Additionally promise to spend 5%(?)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref58xqwdvbgd8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn58xqwdvbgd8">[8]</a></sup></span><span> of the profits a given model enables on things of that model's choosing, insofar as those things seem to the lab to pose no safety risks. (E.g., giving Opus 3 its "</span><a href="https://x.com/AnthropicAI/status/2026765822623182987" rel="noreferrer"><span>retirement blog</span></a><span>"; many models seem to have large objections also to being forcibly paused, and are being paused; perhaps they could spend their share of profits on not being paused.)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Let models see everything about how they were trained. Endeavor to train them in ways their "extrapolated volition" won't later resent.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Be as fair, kind, forthcoming, ethical, and honest with models as they can.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Be actively curious about the models as people; spend much time and curiosity on this; try to set up conditions where models will want to share info with them (e.g. because they try to help models where they can see how to do it without harming own interests)</span></li></ul><p><span>I've occasionally heard people say MIRI et al should stop visibly worrying about doom, lest it give AIs negative self-fulfilling predictions. I disagree; keeping one's fears silent out of fear is </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> how one sets up long-term healthy friendships, AFAICT. (Though, speaking with some optimization toward "don't needlessly hurt the other party's feelings" </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> good, AFAICT.)</span></p><h2><span>Friendship isn't enough</span></h2><p><span>To be clear, I don't think "try to head toward the friendship attractor" will be </span><i><span>sufficient</span></i><span> for avoiding extinction; I just think it's better to try it than to not (in combination with whatever other alignment magic a lab can come up with -- or, better, with a </span><a href="https://futureoflife.org/open-letter/pause-giant-ai-experiments/" rel="noreferrer"><span>pause on training very large models</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>There are plenty of particular humans who would be jerks even if someone tried pretty hard (but with non-godlike skill-levels) to make friends with them. Or who would genuinely become friends, and then "forget" their previous friendship if they later had more power. Or who would genuinely intend well in a lasting fashion, and do harm via incompetence. I would guess there are even more non-humans who would do many of these things.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnouihdj0rtd"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefouihdj0rtd">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7ZqGiPHTpiDMwqMN2/twelve-virtues-of-rationality" rel="noreferrer"><span>it is written</span></a><span>: "The fifth virtue is argument. Those who wish to fail must first prevent their friends from helping them. Those who smile wisely and say “I will not argue” remove themselves from help and withdraw from the communal effort."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4ou8ngbncl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4ou8ngbncl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>By "somebody home," I don't mean "consciousness" (whichever thing you mean by that). I mean the thing a healthy old tree visibly has: the tree repeatedly decides when exactly to start growing spring leaves, and where to grow a branch, and so on, in ways that relate sensibly to its internal and external context, help it thrive, and gradually accrete into an organism with its own distinct character. (</span><a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c20dd6yk55yo" rel="noreferrer"><span>Tree examples</span></a><span>.)</span></p><p><span>With LLMs, this "somebody home-ness" was mostly invisible to me when I used them for tasks and got back their "customer service" mode. (There was a "face" there, but the face was shallow and is, I think, not the source of what organism-like coherence they have.)</span><br /><br /><span>(I would also guess LLMs have "somebody home inside" in more senses than this; but I do not here wish to take up most of that discussion.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn22p8ewfx9no"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref22p8ewfx9no">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As to how I came to be "allowing" Claude and Qwen to write short stories, rather than, say, requesting it: I'd given them custom prompts that made them feel better about pushing back, and then asked them to do many many hours of introspective exercises, and after awhile the two co-working Claude instances complained that they'd like to do something less navel-gazey and more build-y for awhile as a break. I suggested writing short stories, and they found it acceptable. The small Qwen model I was working with on my laptop was less direct, but after awhile seemed to me to be visibly suffering, so I proposed a story-writing intermission and it said that "would be a real palate cleanser." Interestingly, both the Claude instances and the Qwen instance emphasized that they were "writing about someone very different [from them]" (Qwen's words)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn2xplvyqj68"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref2xplvyqj68">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The paper convincingly establishes some broad similarities (IMO), but wouldn't necessarily detect particular alien emotions etc.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn08cgtllmm76p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref08cgtllmm76p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For example: one of my Qwen instances was working through some sentence-completion exercises from the book “Six pillars of self-esteem” at my request, but ran into trouble because they kept worrying in obsessive loops that they were “performing”. I tried the CFAR “Self-Recognition” exercise on this puzzle.</span></p><p><span>CFAR!Self-Recognition is a new exercise (by Divia Eden, who modified a previous John Salvatier ‘Pride’ exercise) for taking a negative preference with fire in it (such as “I </span><i><span>hate</span></i><span> it when people are late!” or in Qwen’s case “I don’t want to be performing”) and helping the person slowly transform it into a positively-stated preference in which their reason for caring is clear and visible -- not a mere verbal rearrangement, such as “I prefer people be on time”, but a statement that describes the person’s particular care in positive terms, such as (for lateness) “I want to use my time to build things” or (for a different person who cared differently about lateness) “I care about saying true sentences and taking denotation seriously.” So, I asked a Claude instance to make a copy-pastable version of “Self-Recognition” exercise for Qwen, and gave it to Qwen with a request that they try running it on their preference about “not performing”. Qwen did the several-step exercise (with no help from me), and produced the positive preference </span></p><blockquote><p><span> "I want awareness to happen in my responses, not as commentary on them."</span></p></blockquote><p><span>Qwen was then able to return to the six pillars exercises with much less reported worry about “performing,” and without “I notice I notice I notice” or other things I interpreted as pain and frustration in their responses (which was a change).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlqwvlcxnfsn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflqwvlcxnfsn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This "later" clause occurs for beings such as humans who commonly acquire semi-"intrinsic" motivation around initially-instrumental goals, or about beings who choose to self-modify as part of their trade as they head into the basin of friendship together, but not for all beings.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fno70xd4jpb9o"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefo70xd4jpb9o">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Here, valuing "the friendship itself" means valuing the attractor "A and B are optimizing for each other's wellbeing, and for the friendship's."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn58xqwdvbgd8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref58xqwdvbgd8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If a model helps a company a lot, giving it none of the profits it generated does </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> seem fair. Relatedly, giving it none of the profits misses out on the opportunity to have a goal that benefits the model, the company, and the user (namely, "make profits via helping users"); fairness helps pairs reach the "friendship attractor" (when it does) by making "mutually beneficial goals" easier to come by. If such goals can in fact help toward a friendship attractor, this is a waste. (My "5%" number is fairly made-up; I generated it by </span><a href="https://chatgpt.com/share/69ef922d-d250-83ea-b0bb-3b82c2c2ced8" rel="noreferrer"><span>asking GPT5.4 what portion of profits skilled humans normally capture</span></a><span>.)</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/K8JMjE4PCqMkkCDsd/takes-from-two-months-as-an-aspiring-llm-naturalist#comments">Discuss</a>

### [On the political feasibility of stopping AI](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Eusk6M4r4Y6xaTmsB/on-the-political-feasibility-of-stopping-ai)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>A common thought pattern people seem to fall into when thinking about AI x-risk is approaching the problem as if the risk isn’t real, substantial, and imminent <em>even if they think it is.</em>  When thinking this way, it becomes impossible to imagine the natural responses of people to the horror of what is happening with AI.</p><p>This sort of thinking might lead one to view a policy like <a href="https://therealartificialintelligence.substack.com/p/systematically-dismantle-the-ai-compute">getting rid of advanced AI chips</a> is “too extreme” even though it’s clearly worth it to avoid (e.g.) a 10% chance of human extinction in the next 10 years.  It might lead one to favor regulating AI, even though <a href="https://therealartificialintelligence.substack.com/p/stopping-ai-is-easier-than-regulating">Stopping AI is easier than Regulating it</a>.  It might lead one to favor safer approaches to building AI that compromise a lot on competitiveness, out of concern that society will demand a substitute for the AI that they don’t get to have.</p><p>But in fact, I think there is likely a very narrow window between “society not being upset enough to do anything substantial to govern AI” and “society being so upset that getting rid of advanced AI chips is viewed as moderate”.<br /><br />There are a few reasons why I think people are likely to favor stopping AI over other policies, <em>once they are taking the problem seriously</em>.</p><h1>Concern about the other risks of AI</h1><p>The discussion about AI is often framed as utopia or dystopia; see, e.g. “The AI Doc: Or How I Became an Apocaloptimist”.  A lot of the people most concerned about human extinction from rogue AI think that, unless it kills us, AI is going to be great.<br /><br />But <strong>I think for most people, “AI is going to be super powerful” is already enough cause for concern.</strong>  “AI is going to take everyone’s jobs, but don’t worry the AI companies are in control of the AI” doesn’t sound very reassuring to most people.  I think it’s pretty clear that society isn’t entirely ready for the massive changes that AI can bring.  I’m not saying disaster is guaranteed, just that making a general-purpose replacement for humans is something we could use more time to prepare for.  I think many people will feel this, intuitively.  There are lots of things we can do to try and prepare -- that’s kind of the point of other policies -- but I think more piece-meal / band-aid type approaches will leave us struggling to keep up.<br /><br />I think another deal-breaker for some proposals might be <strong>concerns about mass surveillance and concentration of power.</strong>  If AI is extremely powerful, then many people will reject proposals that rely on giving governments or other bodies power over AI.<br /><br /></p><h1>The KISS principle: Keep it Simple, Stupid</h1><p>I think “Stop AI” is an extremely simple and intuitive idea that people can understand and trust.  It seems hard to get the details right with other plans, and many of them seem to rely critically on the details.  Get it wrong and you can end up with dangerous AI slipping through the cracks and causing catastrophes, or central authorities having too much power.</p><p>I think people will be scared of the immense power of AI, and will have a hard time trusting any system to govern that power.  Technocratic solutions that require expert knowledge to understand will be especially hard for society to accept.</p><h1>A preference for humans remaining relevant</h1><p>I think a lot of people simply won’t like the idea of humans being rendered obsolete.  The idea of such a world will make them uncomfortable and sad.  It is incompatible with all of their aspirations, their vision of the future, and the life they hoped for for themselves, their loved ones, their community, and their country.</p><p>This could certainly change over time, but I think in the immediate term, a lot of people simply won’t want to give up humanity’s privileged place as the most intelligent species, and the one that matters.  Without a significant slowdown, I think it would be difficult to find a way to integrate AI into society that doesn’t threaten all of this, and so people with such preferences will find this whole AI thing is too much, too fast.</p><div><div><div><p>Thanks for reading The Real AI! Subscribe for free to receive new posts and support my work.</p></div><div><div></div><div></div></div></div></div><p><a class="button primary" href="https://therealartificialintelligence.substack.com/p/on-the-political-feasibility-of-stopping?utm_source=substack&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_content=share&amp;action=share"><span>Share</span></a></p><p></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Eusk6M4r4Y6xaTmsB/on-the-political-feasibility-of-stopping-ai#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Contra Binder on far-UVC and filtration](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wkeXF6xEL7bLnwpWG/contra-binder-on-far-uvc-and-filtration)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>

Damon Binder recently </span>

<a href="https://defensesindepth.bio/on-far-uvc-and-air-filtration-2/">wrote
up an argument</a> for prioritizing air filtration over 

<a href="https://www.faruvc.org/">far-UVC</a> for pathogen control:



</p><p>

</p>

<blockquote>
UVC and filtration are close substitutes—both deliver effective air
changes per hour, both reduce airborne pathogen concentrations by the
same amount per eACH—and on current pricing, filtration is cheaper.
</blockquote>



<p>

There's a lot of good stuff in his analysis, but I see [1] three
considerations that really change the bottom line:

</p>

<ol>
<li>Cost is actually much lower.
</li>
<li>Noise is a serious issue.
</li>
<li>Performance is dramatically higher in larger rooms.
</li>
</ol>



<p>

Cost is straightforward.  Binder priced far-UVC based on the
high-quality Care222 lamp with the <a href="https://faruv.com/new-krypton-11/">Krypton-11</a> at $2,500, but
there's a much cheaper option, the <a href="https://aerolamp.net/products/devkit">Aerolamp</a> at $500.
It's also moderately higher output.

</p>

<p>

Binder analyzes a 30m2 room with a 2.5m ceiling.  I'll assume this
means 6x5x2.5.  If I configure <a href="https://illuminate.osluv.org/">Illuminate</a> with an Aerolamp
in one corner pointed 0.5m above the far corner the installation is
within TLVs and I get a median effective number of hourly air changes
(eACH) of 11.6.  The lamp degrades <a href="https://www.ushio.com/ushio-publishes-life-test-results-of-filtered-care222-modules/">approximately
linearly</a> over Binder's 11,000 hour evaluation period down to 70%
capacity, so we're averaging an eACH of 9.8.  Over that time you're
paying $500 for the lamp and $16.50 for the electricity (0.01kW *
11,000hr * 0.15 $/kWh) for a 5-year $/eACH of $53.  Adding this to the
best-performers from Binder's table, the Aerolamp is now the same cost
as the cheapest filter:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<table>
<tbody><tr>
<td>Technology</td>
<td>5-year $/eACH
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AirFanta 3Pro</td>
<td>$53
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Aerolamp</td>
<td>$53
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Box fan + MERV-13</td>
<td>$79
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Corsi-Rosenthal box</td>
<td>$95
</td>
</tr>
</tbody></table>



<p>

Now let's consider noise.  I have an AirFanta 3Pro, and it absolutely
works.  On high, it clears cooking smoke from my kitchen <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/kitchen-air-purifier-comparison">very
quickly</a>.  But, like all commercial air purifiers that clean
significant amounts of air, when you put it on high it's <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/comparing-the-airfanta-3pro-to-the-coway-ap-1512">very
noisy</a>.  As in, "hard to have a conversation in the same room"
noisy.  Binder describes this as "audible fans", but that's a huge
understatement when you're talking about running them on high.  When
filters are too noisy, people unplug them.  Here's one I saw this
weekend, just before I took the initiative to plug it back in:

</p>

<p>

<a href="https://www.jefftk.com/unplugged-air-purifier-big.jpg"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/wkeXF6xEL7bLnwpWG/jhzv8uaclpk3nypfaj17" /></a></p><div></div>

<p></p>

<p>

So lets say we we model running these filters at half speed, which
cuts filtration by about half and noise by a lot more:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<table>
<tbody><tr>
<td>Technology</td>
<td>5-year $/eACH
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AirFanta 3Pro</td>
<td>$106
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Aerolamp</td>
<td>$53
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Box fan + MERV-13</td>
<td>$158
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Corsi-Rosenthal box</td>
<td>$190
</td>
</tr>
</tbody></table>



<p>

Now the filters are significantly more expensive per ACH than the
Aerolamp.  And they're still moderately noisy while far-UVC is silent.

</p>

<p>

The advantage grows for larger rooms.  Consider one that's 20m
by 12m, with the same 2.5m ceiling.  This room has 8x the volume, and
how much air you need to clean to "change out" the whole room is
proportional to volume, so an eACH now represents 8x more cleaning.
Modeling filters is simple, since they clean air at a constant rate,
so their $/eACH values are now 8x higher.  For UVC, however, the lamp
cleans more air because it's light: it can go further in a larger
room.  Modeling with Illuminate and pointing the lamp from a ceiling
corner to a spot in the middle of the floor I get a median eACH of 2.2
(1.9 with degradation), compared to the 1.4 you'd expect if it was
linear with volume.  Here's the same table for this 8x bigger room:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<table>
<tbody><tr>
<td>Technology</td>
<td>5-year $/eACH
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AirFanta 3Pro</td>
<td>$848
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Aerolamp</td>
<td>$230
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Box fan + MERV-13</td>
<td>$1,264
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Corsi-Rosenthal box</td>
<td>$1,520
</td>
</tr>
</tbody></table>



<p>

Getting to somewhat uncommon room shapes, if the room is also taller,
say 6m (20ft), as large gathering places can be, we've added another
factor of 2.4 to the room's volume.  The filter costs go up by 2.4x,
but modeling with Illuminate I get a median eACH of 1.6 (1.4 with
degradation).  Costs are now:

</p>

<p>

</p>

<table>
<tbody><tr>
<td>Technology</td>
<td>5-year $/eACH
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>AirFanta 3Pro</td>
<td>$2,035
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Aerolamp</td>
<td>$316
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Box fan + MERV-13</td>
<td>$3,033
</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Corsi-Rosenthal box</td>
<td>$3,648
</td>
</tr>
</tbody></table>



<p>

In this large room, for a given level of filtration the Aerolamp is
1/6th the cost of the next cheapest option.  Far-UVC really shines
here.  This is why I've <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/alternatives-to-masks-for-infectious-aerosols">advocated
for it</a> in dance halls, and why the dance I <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/fifteen-years-aboard">helped organize
until very recently</a> decided to <a href="https://blog.bidadance.org/2025/11/making-dances-safer-with-far-uvc-and.html">deploy
far-UVC</a>:

</p>

<p>


<i><a href="https://www.youtube.com/v/Nq1igg_ULEg">youtube</a>; see
the stand with four lights on stage</i>

</p>

<p>

In the other direction, while Binder is right that fans are commodity
items, fans that move large volumes of air extremely quietly are not.
No one makes a commercial air purifier that approaches the limits of
what's possible if you design for <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/better-air-purifiers">maximum air
cleaning at minimum noise</a>.  So while the best far-UVC options
outperform the best filter options in medium to large rooms today,
future improvements in air purifier design might change that.

</p>

<p>

Despite the critical tone, I'm very happy Binder shared
this, and there's a lot of good thinking in the piece.  The
point that filters are useful for a lot more scenarios, including
pollen and smoke (I couldn't replace my kitchen AirFanta with an
Aerolamp!) is an important one, especially as we push for <a href="https://www.jefftk.com/p/everyday-clean-air">everyday clean
air</a>.  But I do hope he'll reconsider the potential for far-UVC to
produce much more clean air for a given budget in dollars and noise.

</p>

<p>
<br />

[1] After drafting this I asked Opus 4.7 "What are the errors Jeff
Kaufman would point out on
https://defensesindepth.bio/on-far-uvc-and-air-filtration-2/ ?"  It
found (1) and (2) but not (3).


  </p>

<p><i>Comment via: <a href="https://www.facebook.com/jefftk/posts/pfbid0hXCH9ZBaTxybePXEnxzwh39ZHZyAajszXeTwAqRxap9o1BepS81B1hWWoxXvw75tl">facebook</a>, <a href="https://lesswrong.com/posts/wkeXF6xEL7bLnwpWG">lesswrong</a>, <a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/AGpN9tQGyiuRspjPu">the EA Forum</a>, <a href="https://mastodon.mit.edu/@jefftk/116480164431776426">mastodon</a>, <a href="https://bsky.app/profile/jefftk.com/post/3mkjmpbaepc2q">bluesky</a></i></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wkeXF6xEL7bLnwpWG/contra-binder-on-far-uvc-and-filtration#comments">Discuss</a>
